Yeah… very much. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.

I used to really look forward to choir, for one. Not just the singing, but seeing everybody, having somewhere to be, feeling like I was part of something. And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.

Now it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.

This reduces the likelihood of non-depressive diagnoses such as F48.0 (neurotic depression in ICD-10, which is not typically characterized by full anhedonia or fatigue), and rules out non-psychiatric fatigue codes (R53.81, R53.82, R53.83, R53.9) unless comorbid. Dementia (F03.90, F03.91) and schizophrenia (F20.4) are less likely given the clinical presentation focused on mood and motivation rather than cognitive decline or psychotic symptoms. Recurrent depressive disorder (F33.x) remains as a possible alternative to a single episode (F32.x).",
  "is_final": false
}

Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.

And most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.

So… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.

And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, like losing or gaining a noticeable amount without trying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure, honestly. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.

Some days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.

My wife might notice more than I do, probably. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.

It’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Like there’s resistance in my arms and legs. I notice myself just sitting there longer than I mean to because getting up feels like this whole process.

And talking is like that too. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.

I don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.

I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.\n\nSome days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.\n\nMy wife might notice more than I do, probably. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. This feels different in a way I don’t really know how to explain neatly.

Lately I wake up and it’s just there immediately, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even done anything. Like I open my eyes and already it feels like the whole day is too much, like there’s no point to any of it. It’s not even always connected to a specific thought at first. It’s just… there.

And some days I end up sitting out in the garage by myself and I’ll just start crying. I don’t even always know what started it. I’ll be sitting there, not really doing anything, and then it comes over me, and I’m crying like something terrible just happened, except I can’t point to the one thing. It’s embarrassing to say that. I’m 54 years old and I’m sitting in the garage crying and I can’t even give you a clear reason why.

So, no… I don’t think this is just the same as times I’ve been sad before. This feels heavier. More hopeless. Like it’s gotten into everything.

Not like “I had a long day” tired, but like my whole body has been drained out somehow. Even getting from the bedroom to the kitchen feels ridiculous, like I’ve been hauling lumber all day or doing heavy work, when I haven’t done anything. I’ll sit down or lie down thinking maybe I just need to rest, but it doesn’t really give me anything back. I still feel just as heavy and exhausted after. It’s gotten to where normal little things feel like too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you normally like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nI used to really look forward to choir, for one. Not just the singing, but seeing everybody, having somewhere to be, feeling like I was part of something. And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, like losing or gaining a noticeable amount without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to the scale or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t exactly been checking myself in the mirror much.\n\nFood and weight… I don’t know. It’s all kind of blurred together lately. If I’ve lost or gained, nobody’s said anything to me, and I haven’t noticed some big change like needing a different belt hole or anything like that. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but not anything obvious that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently, like eating a lot less or a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.\n\nSome days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.\n\nMy wife might notice more than I do, probably. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Like there’s resistance in my arms and legs. I notice myself just sitting there longer than I mean to because getting up feels like this whole process.\n\nAnd talking is like that too. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like this kind of depression or low mood, or is this something that’s new for you?\n\nPatient: I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. This feels different in a way I don’t really know how to explain neatly.\n\nLately I wake up and it’s just there immediately, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even done anything. Like I open my eyes and already it feels like the whole day is too much, like there’s no point to any of it. It’s not even always connected to a specific thought at first. It’s just… there.\n\nAnd some days I end up sitting out in the garage by myself and I’ll just start crying. I don’t even always know what started it. I’ll be sitting there, not really doing anything, and then it comes over me, and I’m crying like something terrible just happened, except I can’t point to the one thing. It’s embarrassing to say that. I’m 54 years old and I’m sitting in the garage crying and I can’t even give you a clear reason why.\n\nSo, no… I don’t think this is just the same as times I’ve been sad before. This feels heavier. More hopeless. Like it’s gotten into everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F32.2", "F32.3", "F33.2", "F33.3"],
  "note": "This turn ruled out F32.1 and F33.1 due to the patient describing slowed movement and a heavy, slowed-down feeling, which aligns more with psychomotor retardation rather than the absence of a significant observable change in activity level. The mood remains consistently depressed and hopeless, supporting moderate to severe depressive episode codes.",
  "is_final": false
}

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.

I mean, I’ve had stretches in my life where I was unhappy or stressed, or where things felt heavier for a while, but I can’t really point to another time where it was like this and went on for weeks and really took over my day-to-day life. Not that I remember clearly, anyway.

Part of me hesitates because I don’t want to give you the wrong answer. I’m trying to think back, like, was there some period years ago where I felt bad and just pushed through it and didn’t call it anything? Maybe. But if you mean this kind of deep, stuck feeling that changes how I function, then no, I don’t remember having something like that before. This feels new to me, or at least new in how intense it is.

And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, like losing or gaining a noticeable amount without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to the scale or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t exactly been checking myself in the mirror much.\n\nFood and weight… I don’t know. It’s all kind of blurred together lately. If I’ve lost or gained, nobody’s said anything to me, and I haven’t noticed some big change like needing a different belt hole or anything like that. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but not anything obvious that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently, like eating a lot less or a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.\n\nSome days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.\n\nMy wife might notice more than I do, probably. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like this kind of depression or low mood, or is this something that’s new for you?\n\nPatient: I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Like there’s resistance in my arms and legs. I notice myself just sitting there longer than I mean to because getting up feels like this whole process.\n\nAnd talking is like that too. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like this kind of depression or low mood, or is this something that’s new for you?\n\nPatient: I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. This feels different in a way I don’t really know how to explain neatly.\n\nLately I wake up and it’s just there immediately, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even done anything. Like I open my eyes and already it feels like the whole day is too much, like there’s no point to any of it. It’s not even always connected to a specific thought at first. It’s just… there.\n\nAnd some days I end up sitting out in the garage by myself and I’ll just start crying. I don’t even always know what started it. I’ll be sitting there, not really doing anything, and then it comes over me, and I’m crying like something terrible just happened, except I can’t point to the one thing. It’s embarrassing to say that. I’m 54 years old and I’m sitting in the garage crying and I can’t even give you a clear reason why.\n\nSo, no… I don’t think this is just the same as times I’ve been sad before. This feels heavier. More hopeless. Like it’s gotten into everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time before this one where you felt similarly depressed, where it lasted for weeks or more and affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stretches in my life where I was unhappy or stressed, or where things felt heavier for a while, but I can’t really point to another time where it was like this and went on for weeks and really took over my day-to-day life. Not that I remember clearly, anyway.\n\nPart of me hesitates because I don’t want to give you the wrong answer. I’m trying to think back, like, was there some period years ago where I felt bad and just pushed through it and didn’t call it anything? Maybe. But if you mean this kind of deep, stuck feeling that changes how I function, then no, I don’t remember having something like that before. This feels new to me, or at least new in how intense it is.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in your life when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about four months, since my wife went back to full-time work and I started spending most weekdays alone.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, this time, yes. It’s been going on for about four months now, I think.

It sort of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, there was more movement in the house, more structure, I guess. Then suddenly most weekdays I was alone for long stretches, and that’s when it really seemed to settle in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I was bored or having a hard time with the quiet, but it didn’t lift. It just kept going.

So if you mean something lasting at least two weeks and affecting daily life, then yes… this has been way longer than that. Around four months. And it’s tied pretty closely in my mind to being by myself during the week after she went back to full-time work.

Even getting from the bedroom to the kitchen feels ridiculous, like I’ve been hauling lumber all day or doing heavy work, when I haven’t done anything. I’ll sit down or lie down thinking maybe I just need to rest, but it doesn’t really give me anything back. I still feel just as heavy and exhausted after. It’s gotten to where normal little things feel like too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you normally like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nI used to really look forward to choir, for one. Not just the singing, but seeing everybody, having somewhere to be, feeling like I was part of something. And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, like losing or gaining a noticeable amount without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to the scale or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t exactly been checking myself in the mirror much.\n\nFood and weight… I don’t know. It’s all kind of blurred together lately. If I’ve lost or gained, nobody’s said anything to me, and I haven’t noticed some big change like needing a different belt hole or anything like that. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but not anything obvious that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently, like eating a lot less or a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.\n\nSome days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.\n\nMy wife might notice more than I do, probably. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like it’s gotten into everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time before this one where you felt similarly depressed, where it lasted for weeks or more and affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stretches in my life where I was unhappy or stressed, or where things felt heavier for a while, but I can’t really point to another time where it was like this and went on for weeks and really took over my day-to-day life. Not that I remember clearly, anyway.\n\nPart of me hesitates because I don’t want to give you the wrong answer. I’m trying to think back, like, was there some period years ago where I felt bad and just pushed through it and didn’t call it anything? Maybe. But if you mean this kind of deep, stuck feeling that changes how I function, then no, I don’t remember having something like that before. This feels new to me, or at least new in how intense it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, this time, yes. It’s been going on for about four months now, I think.\n\nIt sort of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, there was more movement in the house, more structure, I guess. Then suddenly most weekdays I was alone for long stretches, and that’s when it really seemed to settle in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I was bored or having a hard time with the quiet, but it didn’t lift. It just kept going.\n\nSo if you mean something lasting at least two weeks and affecting daily life, then yes… this has been way longer than that. Around four months. And it’s tied pretty closely in my mind to being by myself during the week after she went back to full-time work.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a time in your life before this current period where you felt this level of depression—so down, hopeless, or losing interest in things—for at least two weeks and it affected your daily life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Before this current stretch… no, I don’t think so. I’ve been trying to really look back, because I don’t want to just say “no” too quickly and then realize I forgot something important, but I can’t remember another time that felt like this level of depression.

I’ve had bad times, sure. I’ve been discouraged, stressed, disappointed, all of that. There were times where I wasn’t happy with where things were in my life. But I don’t remember it taking over like this, where it went on and on and actually changed how I was getting through the day.

So… as far as I can tell, no. Not before this. This current period feels like the first time it’s been this deep and this hard to shake.

I’ll sit down or lie down thinking maybe I just need to rest, but it doesn’t really give me anything back. I still feel just as heavy and exhausted after. It’s gotten to where normal little things feel like too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you normally like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much. And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, like losing or gaining a noticeable amount without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to the scale or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t exactly been checking myself in the mirror much.\n\nFood and weight… I don’t know. It’s all kind of blurred together lately. If I’ve lost or gained, nobody’s said anything to me, and I haven’t noticed some big change like needing a different belt hole or anything like that. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but not anything obvious that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently, like eating a lot less or a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.\n\nSome days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.\n\nMy wife might notice more than I do, probably. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Like there’s resistance in my arms and legs. I notice myself just sitting there longer than I mean to because getting up feels like this whole process.\n\nAnd talking is like that too. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like this kind of depression or low mood, or is this something that’s new for you?\n\nPatient: I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Like there’s resistance in my arms and legs. I notice myself just sitting there longer than I mean to because getting up feels like this whole process.\n\nAnd talking is like that too. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like this kind of depression or low mood, or is this something that’s new for you?\n\nPatient: I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. This feels different in a way I don’t really know how to explain neatly.\n\nLately I wake up and it’s just there immediately, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even done anything. Like I open my eyes and already it feels like the whole day is too much, like there’s no point to any of it. It’s not even always connected to a specific thought at first. It’s just… there.\n\nAnd some days I end up sitting out in the garage by myself and I’ll just start crying. I don’t even always know what started it. I’ll be sitting there, not really doing anything, and then it comes over me, and I’m crying like something terrible just happened, except I can’t point to the one thing. It’s embarrassing to say that. I’m 54 years old and I’m sitting in the garage crying and I can’t even give you a clear reason why.\n\nSo, no… I don’t think this is just the same as times I’ve been sad before. This feels heavier. More hopeless. Like it’s gotten into everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time before this one where you felt similarly depressed, where it lasted for weeks or more and affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stretches in my life where I was unhappy or stressed, or where things felt heavier for a while, but I can’t really point to another time where it was like this and went on for weeks and really took over my day-to-day life. Not that I remember clearly, anyway.\n\nPart of me hesitates because I don’t want to give you the wrong answer. I’m trying to think back, like, was there some period years ago where I felt bad and just pushed through it and didn’t call it anything? Maybe. But if you mean this kind of deep, stuck feeling that changes how I function, then no, I don’t remember having something like that before. This feels new to me, or at least new in how intense it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, this time, yes. It’s been going on for about four months now, I think.\n\nIt sort of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, there was more movement in the house, more structure, I guess. Then suddenly most weekdays I was alone for long stretches, and that’s when it really seemed to settle in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I was bored or having a hard time with the quiet, but it didn’t lift. It just kept going.\n\nSo if you mean something lasting at least two weeks and affecting daily life, then yes… this has been way longer than that. Around four months. And it’s tied pretty closely in my mind to being by myself during the week after she went back to full-time work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current period where you felt this level of depression—so down, hopeless, or losing interest in things—for at least two weeks and it affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: Before this current stretch… no, I don’t think so. I’ve been trying to really look back, because I don’t want to just say “no” too quickly and then realize I forgot something important, but I can’t remember another time that felt like this level of depression.\n\nI’ve had bad times, sure. I’ve been discouraged, stressed, disappointed, all of that. There were times where I wasn’t happy with where things were in my life. But I don’t remember it taking over like this, where it went on and on and actually changed how I was getting through the day.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no. Not before this. This current period feels like the first time it’s been this deep and this hard to shake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not really sure how to answer that beyond what I’ve been saying. I can’t think of a past episode like that, even a smaller one, that clearly lasted two weeks or more and really interfered with my life.

I mean, I’ve had rough patches. I don’t want to make it sound like my life was always fine before this, because it wasn’t. There were times I felt low or frustrated or kind of stuck, but when I try to compare them to what you’re asking—something that went on for weeks and affected my daily routine or responsibilities—I just don’t have a clear memory of that happening before.

I know you’re asking the same kind of question, and I don’t mean to be difficult… I just keep coming back to the same answer, which is that I really can’t identify a time before this one that fits that description.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’m just completely worn out. Not like “I had a long day” tired, but like my whole body has been drained out somehow. Even getting from the bedroom to the kitchen feels ridiculous, like I’ve been hauling lumber all day or doing heavy work, when I haven’t done anything. I’ll sit down or lie down thinking maybe I just need to rest, but it doesn’t really give me anything back. I still feel just as heavy and exhausted after. It’s gotten to where normal little things feel like too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you normally like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nI used to really look forward to choir, for one. Not just the singing, but seeing everybody, having somewhere to be, feeling like I was part of something. And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, like losing or gaining a noticeable amount without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to the scale or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t exactly been checking myself in the mirror much.\n\nFood and weight… I don’t know. It’s all kind of blurred together lately. If I’ve lost or gained, nobody’s said anything to me, and I haven’t noticed some big change like needing a different belt hole or anything like that. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but not anything obvious that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently, like eating a lot less or a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.\n\nSome days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.\n\nMy wife might notice more than I do, probably. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Like there’s resistance in my arms and legs. I notice myself just sitting there longer than I mean to because getting up feels like this whole process.\n\nAnd talking is like that too. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like this kind of depression or low mood, or is this something that’s new for you?\n\nPatient: I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. This feels different in a way I don’t really know how to explain neatly.\n\nLately I wake up and it’s just there immediately, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even done anything. Like I open my eyes and already it feels like the whole day is too much, like there’s no point to any of it. It’s not even always connected to a specific thought at first. It’s just… there.\n\nAnd some days I end up sitting out in the garage by myself and I’ll just start crying. I don’t even always know what started it. I’ll be sitting there, not really doing anything, and then it comes over me, and I’m crying like something terrible just happened, except I can’t point to the one thing. It’s embarrassing to say that. I’m 54 years old and I’m sitting in the garage crying and I can’t even give you a clear reason why.\n\nSo, no… I don’t think this is just the same as times I’ve been sad before. This feels heavier. More hopeless. Like it’s gotten into everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time before this one where you felt similarly depressed, where it lasted for weeks or more and affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stretches in my life where I was unhappy or stressed, or where things felt heavier for a while, but I can’t really point to another time where it was like this and went on for weeks and really took over my day-to-day life. Not that I remember clearly, anyway.\n\nPart of me hesitates because I don’t want to give you the wrong answer. I’m trying to think back, like, was there some period years ago where I felt bad and just pushed through it and didn’t call it anything? Maybe. But if you mean this kind of deep, stuck feeling that changes how I function, then no, I don’t remember having something like that before. This feels new to me, or at least new in how intense it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, this time, yes. It’s been going on for about four months now, I think.\n\nIt sort of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, there was more movement in the house, more structure, I guess. Then suddenly most weekdays I was alone for long stretches, and that’s when it really seemed to settle in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I was bored or having a hard time with the quiet, but it didn’t lift. It just kept going.\n\nSo if you mean something lasting at least two weeks and affecting daily life, then yes… this has been way longer than that. Around four months. And it’s tied pretty closely in my mind to being by myself during the week after she went back to full-time work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current period where you felt this level of depression—so down, hopeless, or losing interest in things—for at least two weeks and it affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: Before this current stretch… no, I don’t think so. I’ve been trying to really look back, because I don’t want to just say “no” too quickly and then realize I forgot something important, but I can’t remember another time that felt like this level of depression.\n\nI’ve had bad times, sure. I’ve been discouraged, stressed, disappointed, all of that. There were times where I wasn’t happy with where things were in my life. But I don’t remember it taking over like this, where it went on and on and actually changed how I was getting through the day.\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, no. Not before this. This current period feels like the first time it’s been this deep and this hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression in the past that lasted for at least two weeks and affected your daily life, even if it wasn’t as severe or long-lasting as this current one?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that beyond what I’ve been saying. I can’t think of a past episode like that, even a smaller one, that clearly lasted two weeks or more and really interfered with my life.\n\nI mean, I’ve had rough patches. I don’t want to make it sound like my life was always fine before this, because it wasn’t. There were times I felt low or frustrated or kind of stuck, but when I try to compare them to what you’re asking—something that went on for weeks and affected my daily routine or responsibilities—I just don’t have a clear memory of that happening before.\n\nIt’s possible I’m missing something, I guess. Sometimes when you’re in the middle of life you don’t label things that way. But as best as I can tell, no, I don’t think I had a real episode like that before this current one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this current one where you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking the same kind of question, and I don’t mean to be difficult… I just keep coming back to the same answer, which is that I really can’t identify a time before this one that fits that description.\n\nI’ve had periods where I was unhappy or under stress, or where I didn’t feel great about things in my life, but I don’t remember something lasting at least two weeks where it really got in the way of work, family, hobbies, day-to-day functioning… not in the way you’re asking. Nothing that stands out as, “Yes, that was an episode like this.”\n\nSo, as best as I can tell, no. This current period is the first time I can remember it being that persistent and disruptive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

This current period feels like the first time it’s been this deep and this hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression in the past that lasted for at least two weeks and affected your daily life, even if it wasn’t as severe or long-lasting as this current one?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that beyond what I’ve been saying. I can’t think of a past episode like that, even a smaller one, that clearly lasted two weeks or more and really interfered with my life.\n\nI mean, I’ve had rough patches. I don’t want to make it sound like my life was always fine before this, because it wasn’t. There were times I felt low or frustrated or kind of stuck, but when I try to compare them to what you’re asking—something that went on for weeks and affected my daily routine or responsibilities—I just don’t have a clear memory of that happening before.\n\nIt’s possible I’m missing something, I guess. Sometimes when you’re in the middle of life you don’t label things that way. But as best as I can tell, no, I don’t think I had a real episode like that before this current one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this current one where you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking the same kind of question, and I don’t mean to be difficult… I just keep coming back to the same answer, which is that I really can’t identify a time before this one that fits that description.\n\nI’ve had periods where I was unhappy or under stress, or where I didn’t feel great about things in my life, but I don’t remember something lasting at least two weeks where it really got in the way of work, family, hobbies, day-to-day functioning… not in the way you’re asking. Nothing that stands out as, “Yes, that was an episode like this.”\n\nSo, as best as I can tell, no. This current period is the first time I can remember it being that persistent and disruptive.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a time in your life before this current period where you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like I’ve already answered it, but… no, I really don’t think so. I keep going back through my life in my head, trying to be fair about it, because I know memory can be kind of selective. There were definitely times I was unhappy, or worn down, or not interested in much for a little while, but I can’t point to a clear period before this where it lasted at least two weeks and really affected work or family or hobbies in the way you’re describing.

But this kind of sustained thing—where it changes how I’m functioning day to day—this feels like the first time I can honestly identify that happening. So as best as I know, no, not before this current period.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’m just completely worn out. Not like “I had a long day” tired, but like my whole body has been drained out somehow. Even getting from the bedroom to the kitchen feels ridiculous, like I’ve been hauling lumber all day or doing heavy work, when I haven’t done anything. I’ll sit down or lie down thinking maybe I just need to rest, but it doesn’t really give me anything back. I still feel just as heavy and exhausted after. It’s gotten to where normal little things feel like too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you normally like doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much. That’s actually one of the parts that scares me, because those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nI used to really look forward to choir, for one. Not just the singing, but seeing everybody, having somewhere to be, feeling like I was part of something. And I used to play pickup ball when I could, even if I wasn’t exactly young anymore, you know, just getting out there and moving around and talking trash a little. I liked working on cars too — I could spend half a Saturday messing with something under the hood and feel good about it. And grilling for the family… that was kind of my thing. I’d plan it, get the charcoal right, make sure everybody had what they liked.\n\nNow it’s like… I can’t make myself care enough to do any of it. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming out, and I just stare at the phone. Sometimes I don’t even answer. It’s not that I’m busy with something better. I just don’t feel that pull anymore. Even thinking about showing up feels like too much, and the sad part is I don’t even miss it the way I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I would. It’s not just like I get sad for a little while and then it passes. I wake up with it already there, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even really done anything. It’s like the day is over before it starts.\n\nAnd most days it stays with me. I can try to move around or distract myself, but it’s still there underneath everything. Some days I end up just sitting out in the garage by myself, not even working on anything, just sitting there, and I’ll start crying. Sometimes I don’t even know what set it off. There isn’t always a clear reason. It just comes up, and then I feel embarrassed because I’m a grown man sitting there crying in the garage, but I can’t seem to stop it.\n\nSo… yeah. Down most of the day, nearly every day — that sounds about right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently, like losing or gaining a noticeable amount without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been paying close attention to the scale or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different that I’ve noticed, but I also haven’t exactly been checking myself in the mirror much.\n\nFood and weight… I don’t know. It’s all kind of blurred together lately. If I’ve lost or gained, nobody’s said anything to me, and I haven’t noticed some big change like needing a different belt hole or anything like that. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but not anything obvious that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite recently, like eating a lot less or a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I know that sounds like something I should be able to answer better, but I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way.\n\nSome days I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t really eaten much, but then other times I’ll pick at whatever’s around just because it’s there, not because I’m hungry exactly. It doesn’t feel like a clear “I’m starving all the time” or “I can’t eat at all” kind of thing. It’s more like meals have stopped having much structure for me.\n\nMy wife might notice more than I do, probably. I’m not cooking or planning food the way I used to, so I think that makes it harder for me to tell what’s appetite and what’s just me not bothering. If you’re asking whether I’ve had some obvious big change, like eating twice as much or barely touching food every day, I don’t know that I could say that for sure. I just know food doesn’t feel very important lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes… slowed down, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it feels like everything takes effort now, even little things.\n\nIt’s like my body is moving through mud. I’ll go to stand up, or walk across the room, or get something from the kitchen, and it’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or anything, but it feels like I have to push myself through every step. Like there’s resistance in my arms and legs. I notice myself just sitting there longer than I mean to because getting up feels like this whole process.\n\nAnd talking is like that too. Someone will ask me something, and I hear them. I understand the words. But there’s this delay, like the question has to travel through some thick fog before I can answer. I can feel people waiting, and that makes it worse, because I know I’m taking too long, but the words just don’t come out right away. Sometimes I’ll start and then stop because I can’t get the sentence together.\n\nI don’t know that I’d call it restless. It’s more the opposite. Heavy. Slow. Like everything in me has been turned down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like this kind of depression or low mood, or is this something that’s new for you?\n\nPatient: I’ve felt down before, sure, I mean I think everybody has… but not like this. This feels different in a way I don’t really know how to explain neatly.\n\nLately I wake up and it’s just there immediately, this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even done anything. Like I open my eyes and already it feels like the whole day is too much, like there’s no point to any of it. It’s not even always connected to a specific thought at first. It’s just… there.\n\nAnd some days I end up sitting out in the garage by myself and I’ll just start crying. I don’t even always know what started it. I’ll be sitting there, not really doing anything, and then it comes over me, and I’m crying like something terrible just happened, except I can’t point to the one thing. It’s embarrassing to say that. I’m 54 years old and I’m sitting in the garage crying and I can’t even give you a clear reason why.\n\nSo, no… I don’t think this is just the same as times I’ve been sad before. This feels heavier. More hopeless. Like it’s gotten into everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time before this one where you felt similarly depressed, where it lasted for weeks or more and affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stretches in my life where I was unhappy or stressed, or where things felt heavier for a while, but I can’t really point to another time where it was like this and went on for weeks and really took over my day-to-day life. Not that I remember clearly, anyway.\n\nPart of me hesitates because I don’t want to give you the wrong answer. I’m trying to think back, like, was there some period years ago where I felt bad and just pushed through it and didn’t call it anything? Maybe. But if you mean this kind of deep, stuck feeling that changes how I function, then no, I don’t remember having something like that before. This feels new to me, or at least new in how intense it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for at least two weeks that it affected your daily life, like work, family, or hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, this time, yes. It’s been going on for about four months now, I think.\n\nIt sort of started after my wife went back to working full-time. Before that, there was more movement in the house, more structure, I guess. Then suddenly most weekdays I was alone for long stretches, and that’s when it really seemed to settle in. At first I thought maybe I was just adjusting, like maybe I was bored or having a hard time with the quiet, but it didn’t lift. It just kept going.\n\nSo if you mean something lasting at least two weeks and affecting daily life, then yes… this has been way longer than that. Around four months. And it’s tied pretty closely in my mind to being by myself during the week after she went back to full-time work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life before this current period where you felt this level of depression—so down, hopeless, or losing interest in things—for at least two weeks and it affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: Before this current stretch… no, I don’t think so. I’ve been trying to really look back, because I don’t want to just say “no” too quickly and then realize I forgot something important, but I can’t remember another time that felt like this level of depression.\n\nI’ve had bad times, sure. I’ve been discouraged, stressed, disappointed, all of that. There were times where I wasn’t happy with where things were in my life.
